* **Addendum of 2026-10-16, step 3b (owner order: remove historically grown and redundant tests, reuse test data; tests only, no product code changed):**
  * **Evidence for the removal.** Line and branch coverage of `leg_platform` (the `coverage` package 7.16.2, installed into a throw-away folder outside the repository and used with a configuration outside the repository, so no dependency was added; migrations, tests and the test settings omitted) was measured for the full suite before and after: 6280 statements, 1666 branches, 235 statements and 139 branches missed, 95.29 %, **identical before and after, file by file** (a comparison of the executed lines and branches of every file found no loss; the security-relevant modules are therefore identical). The code of the child processes of `test_production_settings` is not measured by either run. Candidates were searched by three throw-away analyses of the test sources (identical test bodies, tests that share at least three identical assertion statements, tests whose string constants and calls are a subset of another test), by name (removed, superseded, obsolete, legacy) and by reading the acceptance modules against the unit modules. Result: the suite has very little redundancy. The tests of the post-save address notice of D-065 are already the removal pins of D-066 (they assert that the code is gone) and the stage and allow-list tests pin D-070; both stay. **Removed: three tests** (table below); the full table with the reasons and the candidates that were examined and kept is in the working notes of the session (`test-removals.md`, not committed).
  * Removed tests (all three are weaker duplicates of a test that stays; none is a security or authorization test without an identical assertion elsewhere): `test_acceptance_final.PortalSettingsFormTests.test_a_system_administrator_opens_and_saves_the_form` (survivor: `test_portal_settings.PortalSettingsAdminTests.test_the_administrator_edits_the_addresses_with_german_labels`, which also opens the form, saves both addresses and checks the history entry); `test_acceptance_final.PortalSettingsFormTests.test_nobody_can_add_or_delete_the_row` (survivor: `test_portal_settings.PortalSettingsAdminTests.test_nobody_can_add_or_delete_the_row`, which is stricter: delete must answer exactly 403 where the removed test also accepted 404); `test_admin_acceptance.PortalSettingsPageTests.test_an_address_without_https_is_still_refused` (survivor: `test_portal_settings.PortalSettingsAdminTests.test_an_address_without_https_is_refused_with_a_german_message`, which also checks the message). 1833 tests became 1830. `docs/ACCEPTANCE.md` names no test, so no reference changed.
  * **Shared test data:** 73 `TestCase` classes (598 tests) create their accounts once in `setUpTestData` instead of in `setUp` (accounts of the account, admin, profile, second-factor, greeting, email-text, lost-phone and other modules; the client and the login stay in `setUp`). Django 6.1 copies the class-level objects for every test (`django/test/testcases.py`, `TestData`, deep copy; read in the installed source), so no test sees a change of another. Not converted: classes whose set-up sends mails or posts forms (`mail.outbox` is reset for every test), set-ups that assert, and tests that create their accounts in the test body. Not done: reusing one logged-in session per class (`force_login` is about 20 statements; it would save roughly the same again, but changes what each test starts with) and a cheaper flush for `TransactionTestCase` (the flush runs the `post_migrate` handlers; `available_apps` is not meant for project tests).
  * **`TransactionTestCase`:** 17 classes (96 tests) became 16 (95): `RateLimitDatabaseTests` (one test, default database only) is a `TestCase` now. The others are needed: eleven run real threads (names `Concurrent...`, `Parallel...`), `CreateSuperuserTests` and `InitialAdministratorTests` need no open transaction (`create_superuser` refuses to run inside one), `MailWorkerTransactionTests` asserts that no transaction is open, and `DirectoryDatabaseTests` and `RealDirectoryTests` read through a second connection (the mirror `addresses` of `address_import`) that sees only committed rows (verified: converted to `TestCase`, 46 of the 59 tests of `DirectoryDatabaseTests` failed). *(Obsolete since D-075: the directory is an own SQLite file with one connection per alias; both classes are `TestCase` classes again.)* Short comments say why where the reason is not in the name.
  * **`test_production_settings`:** 13 child processes (about 19 s) became four, started together once for the module: production and stage, each with the unchanged configuration (deployment check, values, and for production the password hashing) and with `DJANGO_SECURE_HSTS_INCLUDE_SUBDOMAINS=True`. The refusals at startup (missing email mode, `console`, `allowlist`, a list of allowed recipients) are provoked inside the base process by importing the settings module afresh (`runpy`) with changed variables before Django is set up; they raise the same `ImproperlyConfigured` as the start of the application, and the tests now also assert the exception type and keep the message assertions. Every test and every assertion is kept. The module takes 3.8 s (19.4 s before).
  * **No huge `subTest` matrices exist** (the largest loops are the forms and routes of the security tests, which are not equivalent repeats); nothing was dropped. `--keepdb` is required nowhere: no test, no script and no CI job depends on it (`-KeepDb` of `scripts/test.ps1` stays an opt-in).
  * **Result:** the paired measurement (old and new tests alternately on the same machine, SQL statements counted by a throw-away runner outside the repository): 172,402 to 151,810 SQL statements (-12 %), process CPU time 121.8 s to 112.9 s, test time 184.6 s to 166.9 s; without the production-settings module (the temporary copy of the old tests had only the older three tests of it) 180.4 s to 163.6 s (-9 %); the areas `admin` 31.6 s to 25.7 s, `profile` 47.7 s to 42.9 s, `mail` 25.9 s to 23.0 s, `templates` 70.6 s to 64.9 s, `second-factor` 74.7 s to 69.5 s. With coverage measuring, the full suite took 271.5 s before and 169.8 s and 171.9 s after. The machine was noisy on that afternoon (the same old tests took 151 s and 201 s in two runs, database creation 9 to 10 s instead of 3.3 s), so the single-run figures vary by up to 25 %; the numbers of statements are exact.
  * Not verified: runs with `--shuffle` or in a random order (the converted classes do not depend on the order, but it was not run); the child-process module was run on Windows only.

## D-072: Corrections After the Security Review of D-070 and D-071; Deadlock in the IP Attempt Counter (2026-10-16)

* Date: 2026-10-16
* Status: decided by the main session (team lead) as corrections of review findings (M-1, M-2, L-1 to L-7 of the security review of D-070 and D-071) and of a real defect that step 3b of D-071 reported (Part B). Security-relevant (availability of the confirmations and of the login counters, D-017, D-062); a candidate for the human review of D-019. No product behaviour changes except the lock order described below; no limit is weakened.
* **Part A: findings of the review of D-070 and D-071.**
  * **M-1 (D-070 risks, stage).** Verified in the code (`accounts/views.py` `MailRequestView`, `accounts/mail_queue.py` `submit_mail_request`, `accounts/registration.py` `process_registration`, `accounts/password_reset.py` `process_reset_request`, `accounts/rate_limits.py`): the public registration form makes the sender mail a verification link to any address that has no account, so a stage instance that is reachable from the Internet sends real mail to third parties; the password-reset form mails only active accounts. The limits are those of production (D-038, D-058). D-070 now states three more risks (abuse of the registration form, no way for a recipient to recognise stage mail, replies and bounces in the stage mailbox, D-006) and three mitigations that are **procedures and change no code**: a clear stage marker in `INSTANCE_NAME` (required in the checklist), access protection of the stage host at the reverse proxy (IP allow-list or basic authentication; recommended), and a named reader of the stage sender mailbox. `docs/DEPLOYMENT.md`, section 5.4 (items 5 to 7), `docs/OPERATIONS.md` D13. Not changed: the owner's decision that stage behaves like production.
  * **M-2 (migration note).** `docs/DEPLOYMENT.md`, section 5.3: rollback (the code before D-070 refuses `graph` on stage, so the environment files must be reverted with the code), the order (confirmation of section 5.4, then the files, then the update), and the check and purge of queued `MailJob` rows before the first restart (the worker would send every queued job without restriction). Section 12.3 refers to it (step 1 and "Recovery after a failed update").
  * **L-1 (`--since`).** `scripts/test_areas.py` `verified_revision`: a value that starts with `-` (or contains NUL) is refused; the others are resolved with `git rev-parse --verify --quiet --end-of-options <rev>^{commit}` (verified with the installed git 2.44.0; `--end-of-options` exists since git 2.24, an older git fails and the value is refused) and only the resolved hash reaches `git diff`.
  * **L-2 (labels).** A changed test module whose label does not match `^[A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_.]*$` selects the full suite instead of being passed to `manage.py test`.
  * **L-3 (guard against parallel runs).** `scripts/test.ps1` holds a machine-wide named mutex (`Global\LegPlatformTestRun`; an abandoned mutex of a crashed run is taken over) and keeps the process check for runs started by hand. The documented exit codes are real now (no `Write-Error` under `$ErrorActionPreference = 'Stop'`; verified: no arguments gives 2, a second start while the mutex is held gives 3, a run after the holder ended gives 0). Limits stated in the script and in `docs/STARTUP.md`, section 13: a run started by hand with another command line, a run on another machine against the same MariaDB server and processes of other users that a non-elevated shell cannot read (the script warns) are not detected.
  * **L-4 (weak test hasher).** `leg_platform/test_settings.py` raises `ImproperlyConfigured` unless `APP_ENV=local` (CI and the local runner use `APP_ENV=local`; no test imports the module with another value). New tests in `test_production_settings.py`: the module is refused for `stage` and `production` in a child process and loads for `local`; `manage.py`, `wsgi.py` and `asgi.py` default to `leg_platform.settings` and name no other module. The separate synthetic settings of the address directory tests (`addresses/tests/settings.py`) stay as they are: they load no instance configuration.
  * **L-5 (core files, CI).** `leg_platform/environment.py` is a core file: a change runs the full suite (it builds every security setting, the mail backend, the cookie names and the base URL of the mail links; decided instead of a list of extra areas, because it is rarely changed and its effect reaches mail, login and session tests). `.github/workflows/` runs the `packaging` area, which now includes `test_production_settings`; the new `CiDeploymentCheckTests` keeps the stage step of `ci.yml` equal to the production step except for `APP_ENV`, and the child processes check the real settings for both environments. CI itself and the database part of `check --deploy` still run only in CI (or by hand, `docs/STARTUP.md`, section 13).
  * **L-6 (tests of the runner).** `leg_platform/tests/test_areas.py`: `changed_files()` with fixed git output (NUL separation, names with spaces and quotes, renames and copies, a rename without the old path, `--since` with the resolved hash), `verified_revision` (options, NUL, unknown revision) and against a real throw-away git repository (staged rename with spaces, committed changes since a revision, option and unknown revision refused). A file name that is not valid UTF-8 stops the runner with a clear message ("not valid UTF-8", rename or `-Full`): fail closed, not a guess.
  * **L-7 (documents).** `docs/SECURITY.md`, section 11: the refusal of a non-empty `EMAIL_ALLOWED_RECIPIENTS` outside `local` is listed with the tests. `docs/OPERATIONS.md` D13 says "without need" like D-070. The comment in `test_settings.py` says "at least 600,000 iterations (D-023)"; the installed Django 6.1 default (1,500,000) was verified in its source, the default of the declared minimum (Django 6.0) was **not** verified and is not stated.
* **Part B: the deadlock of unit 3b (`ParallelUseTests.test_parallel_wrong_passwords_of_the_password_forms_stay_under_the_cap`).**
  * **Reproduced.** Running only `leg_platform.accounts.tests.test_second_factor.ParallelUseTests`: MariaDB error 1213 (deadlock) as HTTP 500 in 1 of 30 runs, then in 2 of 16 further runs; the single test alone did not fail in 80 runs (the timing depends on the load of the machine).
  * **Cause (the statement that was the victim is verified by the stack traces of the failed runs; the other side is inferred from the code and the fix is proved by the tests, see below).** All requests of one account take the account row lock first (`select_for_update` in `second_factor.verify_password` / `verify_second_factor`), and under it the IP counter: `reserve_client_ip_attempt` locks the `LoginIpBucket` row and then scans and deletes in `LoginIpAttempt` through the index `(bucket, attempted_at)` (InnoDB locks the index entries first, then the rows). `settle_client_ip_attempt`, which runs **after** the transaction in autocommit, deleted its row by primary key without the lock of the bucket (InnoDB locks the row first, then its index entries). Two parallel requests of one client address (same bucket) could therefore wait for each other: an inversion of the lock order between the index scan of the next request and the delete by primary key of the previous one. Every failed run stopped in the `attempts.delete()` of `settle_client_ip_attempt`. The cleanup command already locked the bucket first (its comment says "locking the bucket first like the requests do"); only `settle` did not. The order account row, then counter rows, then session and Axes writes was not the problem: the counter rows (`SessionCodeFailure`, `ConfirmationFailure`) are only written under the account lock.
  * **Fix (consistent lock order, minimal).** `login_protection.settle_client_ip_attempt` locks the bucket row (`select_for_update`) in a short transaction before it deletes or updates the attempt, so that every statement on the attempts of a bucket runs under the lock of the bucket. Callers that settle inside the transaction of the account lock already hold the bucket lock (the reservation happened in the same transaction), so the order stays account, then bucket, then attempts. One more statement (the bucket id of the attempt) and one lock per settle. Nothing about the counting changes: the limit of the IP address, the account limits and the answers are the same; the settle is only serialized with the reservation, which makes the count more exact, not weaker.
  * **No retry added.** A bounded retry of the whole transaction was considered and rejected: the transactions of the confirmations also change the request (flags, session) and the IP slot, so a repeat would need its own tests and could hide a real fault; a remaining deadlock fails closed as before (an exception, HTTP 500, nothing accepted). **What the counters keep depends on where the settle runs (corrected after the second review, L-1):** where the settle runs inside the transaction of the account lock (`verify_second_factor` and `verify_password` for the lockout, pause and refusal branches and for a wrong entry, and the handler's `user_login_failed` in those branches), an error 1213 rolls back the **whole** transaction, including `record_failed_code`, `record_confirmation_failure` and the use-up of the code; the wrong entry of that request is then **not counted**, and the answer is a 500 that reveals nothing about the entry (no oracle). The effect on the limits is small (one uncounted try in a rare, failed request), and nothing is accepted. Where the settle runs after the commit (the final `settle_client_ip_attempt` of both functions, the login view, `begin_pending_login`), the counted attempt stays: the IP slot stays reserved and pending (and counted by the reservation, `count()`), because a failing settle raises before `del request.login_ip_attempt_id` and before `return device`.
  * **Tests.** `test_login_protection.py`, `ConcurrentLoginIpAttemptTests`: `test_settling_waits_for_the_lock_of_the_bucket` (deterministic: a second connection holds the bucket lock, the settle must wait; **verified to fail in two runs with the lock removed temporarily, and to pass with it**) and `test_parallel_reserve_and_settle_of_one_address_do_not_deadlock` (12 threads, five rounds on one address; a probabilistic guard: with the lock removed it failed in the first probe run, together with the deterministic test, and passed in the second, so it alone is no proof). Result after the fix: 60 runs of `ParallelUseTests` together with `ConcurrentLoginIpAttemptTests`, 60 passed, no deadlock (before: 3 failures in 46 runs of the class).
  * **Open risk (first version; corrected by Part C below).** Other statements outside the account lock that touch counter rows (`forget_confirmation_failures` at logout deletes `SessionCodeFailure` rows of the own session in autocommit) were read and found to touch only rows of one session, so no second resource is involved; a stress test of logout in parallel with confirmations of the same session does not exist. The InnoDB deadlock report (`SHOW ENGINE INNODB STATUS`) could not be read: the application account has no `PROCESS` privilege, and it is not widened for this (D-046, security rule on database privileges). The cause above therefore rests on the stack traces, the lock behaviour of InnoDB and the tests that fail without the lock, not on a lock graph of the server. **The first sentence was wrong (second review, M-1 and L-4); see Part C.**
* **Part C: corrections after the second security review of Part B (2026-10-16; findings M-1, L-1 to L-5; all verified in the code and the models first).**
  * **M-1 (verified: model, migration 0023 and 0022, both commands).** `cleanup_confirmation_failures` ran two bare autocommit `DELETE ... WHERE occurred_at < ...` and `WHERE updated_at < ...` statements without a lock. `ConfirmationFailure` has the indexes `(account, occurred_at)` (`accounts_cf_account_time`) and the foreign key; none starts with `occurred_at`, so the clean-up statement most likely scans the clustered index (clustered row first, then its index entries), while `record_confirmation_failure` deletes the expired rows of one account through `(account, occurred_at)` under the account lock (index entry first, then the row): the same inversion as in the IP counter. `SessionCodeFailure` has `accounts_scf_updated` (`updated_at`) and the unique `(account, session_digest)`; the clean-up reaches rows through the first, the requests through the second. **Fix:** `cleanup_confirmation_failures` follows the order account row, then counter rows. The accounts that have expired rows are read without a lock (`SELECT DISTINCT account_id`); then for each account, in one short transaction, the account row is locked with `select_for_update(skip_locked=True)` and only the expired rows of this account are deleted (an index that starts with the account, like the requests). An account that a request holds is left for the next run (the clean-up never waits for a request, so it cannot be a victim or a partner of a cycle; every failed confirmation also removes the expired rows of its own account); the command reports how many it left. `leg_platform/accounts/management/commands/cleanup_confirmation_failures.py`. The alternative (select the primary keys with `skip_locked`, delete by key in batches) was not chosen: it would lock counter rows without the account row, which is the order that the requests do not use.
  * **L-4 (verified: the callers of `login()` and `logout()`).** `forget_confirmation_failures` (login, logout, and `reset_confirmation_failures` after a correct proof) now takes the lock of the account row in a short transaction before it deletes the rows of the session, so that the order is account row, then counter rows everywhere. **No deadlock with the callers that hold other locks:** none of the callers of `login()` and `logout()` (`views.py`, `profile_views.py`, `second_factor.py`, `session_timeout.py`) holds a lock when it sends the signal (`ATOMIC_REQUESTS` is not set; the IP bucket lock of the reservation and of the settle ends with their own short transactions; the registration view logs in after its transaction); `reset_confirmation_failures` runs inside the account transaction that already holds the lock (no wait, tested). The deletion of an account (`delete_or_deactivate`) locks the account row first. **The fallback branch of `_counter_row`** (`row.save(update_fields=["session_digest"])`, `other.delete()` for a row under a digest of `SECRET_KEY_FALLBACKS`) is only called from `confirmation_refused` and `record_confirmation_failure`, both under the account lock (verified by a search of the whole repository); the review is right that it was in a cycle with the logout, and that cycle is closed by the lock in `forget_confirmation_failures`, not by an extra lock in `_counter_row` (it would be a second lock of the same row in the same transaction, no effect). **Consequence (new):** a logout (and a login) waits for a parallel request of the same account that holds the account lock (a password hash and a few statements). If the wait ran into the lock wait timeout (L-5), the signal raises before the session is flushed: the logout fails with a 500 and the session stays; the person tries again. Fail closed and rare; not changed (a `skip_locked` at the logout would leave the count of a live session undeleted).
  * **L-1.** The wording of the fail-closed behaviour is corrected above (the whole transaction rolls back where the settle runs inside the account transaction; the counted attempt stays where it runs after the commit; no oracle).
  * **L-2 (tests added, all verified to fail when the lock is removed from the settle, the forget or the clean-up: temporary mutation, then restored).** `test_login_protection.py`, `ConcurrentLoginIpAttemptTests`: the settle inside an outer transaction that already holds the bucket (no wait for itself, a parallel reservation waits and continues after the commit; success and failure); the bucket removed by a second connection between the lookup and the lock (no error, nothing left); a forced `OperationalError` (1213) in the settle (raised, not treated as a success, the slot stays pending and the request keeps its attempt, a repeat settles it); the lock-wait test for `succeeded=False` too; the wait check no longer rests on `done.wait(1.0)` alone: the settle reports that it reached the lock (`entered`, asserted first), then it must not be finished one second later and nothing may be changed under the lock. `test_session_code_limit.py`, `ConfirmationCounterLockOrderTests`: the clean-up removes expired rows of every account and keeps the others, skips an account that a second connection holds and never waits (and removes the rows at the next run), takes the account lock with `skip_locked` for each kind; the logout waits for the account lock (with the same `entered` check) and removes only its own session's row; forgetting inside the transaction that holds the lock does not wait; forgetting for a deleted account does not fail. `test_second_factor.py`, `ParallelUseTests`: a stress test of logout in parallel with wrong passwords of the same sessions (6 sessions, a second client with the same session cookie for each, 2 wrong entries each, bounded to a few seconds): no 500, no thread that raised. Like the first probabilistic test it is a guard, not a proof; the deterministic tests are the proof of the lock order.
  * **L-3 (statistical evidence).** The figures of Part B are weak and are not a proof: 1 failure in 30 runs and 2 in 16 further runs before the fix; 0 in 80 runs of the single test; 3 failures in 46 runs of the class before and 0 in 60 after. At a failure rate of about 3 in 46 (6.5 %), 0 failures in 60 runs has a probability of about 2 % by chance, which supports the fix on that machine and under that load only. The rates themselves come from small samples; the timing depends on the machine. What proves the lock order is the deterministic tests that fail without the lock, not the count of runs. The runs after Part C are listed in the report of the unit.
  * **L-5 (lock wait timeout, behaviour).** The settle, the logout and the login take their lock without `nowait` and without `skip_locked`, so they wait for the holder up to `innodb_lock_wait_timeout` (a server variable; its value on the instance was not read, the setting is the operator's) and then fail with error 1205 as an exception: HTTP 500, nothing accepted, the slot stays pending (the settle) or the session stays (the logout). The account lock is held by a check only while it hashes the password or verifies the code, so waits are short. Fail closed, not new; recorded so that a 500 under extreme load is understood.
  * **The other side of the cycle stays inferred (L-3, "not reviewed").** The victim statement (`attempts.delete()` in the settle) is documented from stack traces; the partner (the reservation's `DELETE ... attempted_at < cutoff` reaching recent settled rows only if the optimizer scans more than the range, for example through the foreign key index) is inferred from the code and the lock behaviour of InnoDB, not shown by a lock graph or an `EXPLAIN`. The operator step in `docs/DEPLOYMENT.md`, section 12.4 ("Lock behaviour of the counter statements"), checks the plans of the cleanup and reservation statements on the real database (`EXPLAIN DELETE`; read-only) and names the deadlock report for a privileged session. Not done in development: no `PROCESS` privilege, and the plans of an empty test database say little.
  * **Not covered (stated, not hidden):** `end_all_sessions` and the cascade of an account deletion were not changed (the deletion locks the account row first, `models.py` `delete_or_deactivate`, verified by reading); other users of `select_for_update` outside the confirmation and IP paths were not reviewed. The index list of the tables was read from `models.py` and migrations 0001, 0022 and 0023; the indexes of a real database (`SHOW INDEX`) were not read.
* Tests run (all with `leg_platform.test_settings`): `leg_platform.tests.test_areas`, `test_production_settings` (new classes) and `test_login_protection` as above; the final runs of the affected areas and of the full suite are in the report of the unit.
* Sources: the review text of the security reviewer (2026-10-16); Django documentation "Database transactions" and `select_for_update` (https://docs.djangoproject.com/en/6.1/ref/models/querysets/#select-for-update); git documentation `git-rev-parse` and `gitcli` (`--end-of-options`); `AGENTS.md`, sections 4 and 7; D-006, D-017, D-038, D-040, D-046, D-058, D-062, D-070, D-071. The InnoDB lock behaviour (index entries are locked before the clustered row in a scan, the clustered row before the index entries in a delete by primary key) is the explanation of the stack traces and the probe runs above; it was **not** re-checked in the MariaDB documentation in this unit. Part C: the second security review of Part B (2026-10-16); MariaDB documentation "EXPLAIN" (`EXPLAIN` of `DELETE` shows the plan without running it; `https://mariadb.com/docs/server/reference/sql-statements/administrative-sql-statements/analyze-and-explain-statements/explain`, checked 2026-10-16); Django documentation `select_for_update(skip_locked=True)`.

## D-073: Portal Settings in the Administration: Name, Website, Logos and Colours (Owner Request of 2026-10-16)

* Date: 2026-10-16
* Status: decided by the main session (team lead) on the owner's request; implemented; **not yet reviewed by the security reviewer; a candidate for the human review of D-019** (uploads, a public route, a delegated right, audit). The browser checks listed under "Open" were not done.
* Owner request (2026-10-16): "the settings of the platform such as name, hostname, light and dark logos etc. must be maintained in the Django admin area; a module for all platform-specific configuration; the URLs for the privacy notice and the imprint exist already."
* **Supersedes** (the text of these decisions is kept; read them with this entry): D-060, "Documentation (WL-24)" and `docs/DESIGN.md` 3.2, "branding stays with the deployment"; D-051, "Logos remain self-hosted static images, never inline SVG or uploads" (the images are still never inline; uploads are now allowed under the rules below); `docs/SECURITY.md` section 6, "secure uploads, if introduced later; this includes any later web upload of logo files" (the rules are now in force there); D-061, block 6, point 10 (the box "Erste Schritte" no longer says that name and logo are set at installation). The designer's specification of 2026-10-16 (`instance-settings-spec.md`) is the basis of the page.
* Decision:
  * **The module is the existing singleton `PortalSettings`**, extended (no second model); it stays in the group "Inhalte" and keeps the name "Portal settings" / "Portal-Einstellungen" (the interface says "Portal" everywhere; D-060 removed "Platform"). New fields: `name`, `website_url`, the five colours (`accent_light`, `text_light`, `accent_dark`, `text_dark`, `highlight`) and, per logo, the file, its type and its SHA-256 digest. Migration `0025_portal_settings_branding`: **schema only** (fields, the model options and the choices of the security event); **no data migration, no group, no instance value** (`AGENTS.md`, sections 6 and 9; a test inspects the operations).
  * **Precedence per field: administration (database), then environment, then platform default.** An empty text or colour means "use the server configuration" (stated under every field: "Serverkonfiguration: <value>"). While the row is empty the portal behaves exactly as before. One accessor, `leg_platform.accounts.portal_settings.portal_values(request=None)`, returns an immutable object; it reads `settings.INSTANCE_*` when it is called, works without a request (emails, commands), reads the database at most once per request (and never the logo files), keeps nothing between requests (a change is visible at once), **checks the effective values again when it reads them** (name: at most 100 characters, no C*/Zl/Zp characters; addresses: clean `https://`; the five colours together with the rules below; a logo only with a valid type and digest, both or none) and **never raises**: a failed value or a failing database gives a warning and the server configuration, as `legal_links` did. `legal_links` is now a view of the accessor. All call sites were switched: the template context (`context_processors.instance`, lazy), the administration (`site_header`, `site_title`), `emails.py` and `email_texts.py` (name, contact address, privacy address), the TOTP issuer, the stylesheet `instance.css` (with an ETag and `no-cache`), the error pages and the box "Erste Schritte".
  * **Shared colour validation.** `leg_platform.branding.colour_problems` is the one implementation of the rules (format `#RRGGBB`; the accent at least 4.5:1 on both surfaces of its mode and with its text colour; the highlight at least 4.5:1 with dark text; D-051, D-056). `load_branding` raises `ImproperlyConfigured` from it at the start (the messages are unchanged), the form turns it into per-field messages with the ratio (cut, not rounded, to two decimals), and the accessor uses it when reading. If the accent is stored and the text colour is empty, the text colour is the one of white and `#0B1520` with the higher contrast. The name rule is `environment.is_valid_instance_name`, shared by the start, the model validator and the accessor (it keeps header injection out of email subjects).
  * **Permissions.** `PortalSettings` has the default rights `view` and `change` (no add, no delete), like `EmailText`; they are ordinary rights that a role gets at runtime (D-012; no role is created, named or checked in code, no default role has them; superusers pass automatically). `has_module_permission` follows view or change; the page of a holder of the view right is read only and a post is 403; the history stays for system administrators (it names accounts, as for the email texts, D-039). The group page offers the two rights, names them in the help ("Name, Logo, Farben und Adressen des Portals ändern") and warns that the right changes what every visitor sees and which links the portal shows; the box "Erste Schritte" shows the item "Name, Logo und Farben" ("eigene Einstellungen" or "Serverkonfiguration") to whoever may open the page. The old text "only system administrators can change the portal settings" is removed.
  * **What stays in the environment and why.** The host name, the base address (`INSTANCE_BASE_URL`), `ALLOWED_HOSTS`, the origins of forms, the mail mode and sender, the Microsoft Graph credentials (their own module, system administrators only), the database, the secrets, the retention minimums, the proxy and HSTS settings. The owner named "hostname": a host name that is editable in the database would let a compromised or careless account with the right redirect every security link in an email (`docs/SECURITY.md`, section 4) and weaken `ALLOWED_HOSTS` and the CSRF origins, which are security settings of the server and are checked at the start (D-013, D-025, D-028). They are shown **read only** in a collapsed fieldset "Serverkonfiguration (nur lesen)" **for system administrators only** (least disclosure; a holder of the right sees the rest of the page): the environment, debug mode, base address, allowed host names, trusted origins, mail mode, sender address, the **number** of allowed recipients, the days of the security log, whether the proxy header is set, the number of proxies and HSTS. Never shown: `DB_*`, `ADDRESS_*`, secret keys, the bootstrap address, the addresses of allowed recipients, any password or key (a test checks the page).
  * **Logos: stored in the database, not on the server.** Reasons: no new environment variable, no directory to create, no file permissions, no path (so no traversal and no way to serve a user-chosen path), the backup of the database contains them and a restore brings them back with the settings, one transaction with the other settings, and no second place to forget in a new instance. The costs: the file is in the row (up to 2 MB; the accessor, the box and the page never load it, only the logo view and the replacing save do) and the row grows; MariaDB's `max_allowed_packet` default is far above it (**not measured here**). The files belong to the database backup (`docs/DEPLOYMENT.md`, 12.1). The environment logos (`INSTANCE_LOGO_*`) stay as the fallback until logos are uploaded; both logos or none (the dark logo may need another file, so one cannot stand alone). No `MEDIA_ROOT`, no `MEDIA_URL` route, no `FileField`.
  * **Validation of an upload (`leg_platform/accounts/logo_images.py`; the first of several layers).** The type is decided from the content, never from the name or the content type of the request; the name is never stored or used; the file is read up to 1 MB plus one byte; a file is accepted exactly as it is or rejected, never rewritten. **PNG:** signature, every chunk (length, a chunk name of letters, CRC-32), the header (the allowed colour types and bit depths, 64 to 4000 pixels), palette rules, the data chunks in one run and as one zlib stream that inflates to **exactly** the size the header states (in steps of 64 KB, never more; Adam7 included), `IEND` as the last bytes (so no polyglot with trailing data), no unknown critical chunk; at most 1 MB; the image is **not** decoded or re-encoded. **SVG:** at most 200 KB; the bytes must be UTF-8 without NUL, **must contain no `<!DOCTYPE`, `<!ENTITY` or `<![CDATA[` and no processing instruction except the XML declaration, checked before any parser sees them** (so no entity can be declared, and neither "billion laughs" nor XXE can happen); then the parser of the standard library reads plain elements with at most 20 levels and 2000 elements; only the SVG namespace; the elements `svg g path rect circle ellipse line polyline polygon defs linearGradient radialGradient stop clipPath title desc`; a fixed list of attributes (geometry, `transform`, `viewBox`, the presentation attributes, the gradient attributes, `id`, `style`); a `style` attribute only with declarations of the same presentation properties; every value from a fixed character set (letters, digits, white space and `# % . , ( ) + - _`); `url()` only as `url(#id)` of an id of the same file; a `viewBox` with a positive size is required; no text except in `title` and `desc`. Everything else is rejected, including `script`, `style`, `image`, `foreignObject`, `a`, `use`, animation, filters, `xlink:href`, event handlers and the data of Inkscape or Illustrator. The message names only a sanitised element or attribute name (letters, at most 40 characters, otherwise "unknown").
  * **Parser and library decision (`AGENTS.md`, sections 3 and 7).** No new dependency. **Pillow** is not a dependency (checked in `pyproject.toml` and the virtual environment); it would add a large decoder with a long history of image-parsing vulnerabilities to every instance for a check that the chunk walk and the bounded inflate do without, and the image is never decoded by the server (only browsers decode it, in `<img>`). **defusedxml** is not a dependency; its latest release on PyPI is 0.7.1 and its classifiers end at Python 3.9 (PyPI JSON of the project, checked 2026-10-16), and every attack it defends against (entity expansion, external entities and DTD retrieval) needs a DTD or an entity declaration, which is rejected on the bytes before the parser sees the data; the Python documentation names exactly these as the dangers of its parsers ("XML security", https://docs.python.org/3/library/xml.html, checked 2026-10-16: billion laughs, quadratic blowup, external entities by default not fetched by Expat; "large tokens" fixed in Expat 2.6.0 and mitigated here by the 200 KB limit). The installed Expat is 2.8.5 (`pyexpat.EXPAT_VERSION`, checked 2026-10-16); an older system Expat on a server is covered by the same reasoning. If the project owner prefers a maintained wrapper regardless, `defusedxml.ElementTree.iterparse(..., forbid_dtd=True)` can replace `ElementTree.iterparse` in one line.
  * **Serving (`leg_platform.presentation.instance_logo`, `/branding/logo/<light|dark>/`).** A public view without a session (the logo is on every page, also before the login; its name is in `UNRESTRICTED_VIEWS` and `PASSWORD_CHANGE_VIEWS` of `second_factor.py`, like `instance_stylesheet`, so an account with an incomplete second factor or a forced password change still gets it). Only `light` and `dark` are routed (a path converter); the content comes from the validated column and its type from the stored type; the SHA-256 of the content is compared with the stored digest before it is served; the content type is fixed (`image/png` or `image/svg+xml`, never HTML); headers: `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`, `Content-Security-Policy: sandbox; default-src 'none'`, `Cross-Origin-Resource-Policy: same-origin`, `ETag` (the digest, with 304); the address carries `?v=` and the first 16 characters of the digest, a different `v` is 404, and with `v` the response is `public, max-age=86400` (the address changes with the file), without it `no-cache`; no logo, a failing database or a mismatch of the digest is 404 (the pages then use the logo of the server configuration, which is a static file). GET and HEAD only. The CSP middleware does not overwrite a policy that the view set (verified in the installed Django source, `ContentSecurityPolicyMiddleware`).
  * **No widening of the CSP (decision of the team lead).** No `blob:` source and no `csp_override`: a file that is chosen but not saved is **not previewed**; the saved logos are shown in the preview and the status after saving. The live preview of the colours uses an external script (`static/js/portal-preview.js`) that sets CSS variables through the CSSOM (`element.style.setProperty`) on the two preview panels, and a stylesheet `preview.css` (an admin route that needs the view or change right) for the saved values; the contrast text next to each colour field is advisory and uses the same formula as `branding.contrast` (the constants are compared by a test; the formula was not run against Python in this unit). Without JavaScript everything works and the preview shows the saved values.
  * **Body limit.** The page that takes the logo files is refused above 3 MiB by `RequestBodyLimitMiddleware` before the CSRF middleware reads the body (`BODY_LIMITS`, a test compares the key with the reversed URL); the reverse proxy needs a `3m` location for that path (`docs/DEPLOYMENT.md`, 7.2, 7.3, V35). This also covers the existing Graph upload page only through the proxy (not changed here).
  * **Audit.** `PORTAL_SETTINGS_CHANGED` (long retention, as before) is written once per save with one entry per changed field in the log line: the old and the new name as JSON strings (control characters are escaped, so a value cannot add a log line), `host old -> host new` for addresses, the old and the new colour (or "colours reset to server configuration"), and for a logo `logo_light replaced (png, 48213 bytes, sha256 3fa9c1d2e4b5)` or `removed`, **never the content of a file**. New event `portal_upload_rejected` (migration of the choices, as in 0017): one per request, written by the form, with the reason codes only (for example "svg: element not allowed"); it is not long-kept. The history of the administration (`LogEntry`) records the changed field names and stays for system administrators.
  * **Other points.** The TOTP issuer of a new enrolment is the effective name (existing enrolments keep theirs; cosmetic). The 500 page keeps the environment-only name and logo (it must not query the database, D-054); the 403, 404 and failed-CSRF pages use the accessor and fall back to the environment when the database does not answer (the error-page tag decides by the presence of a request in the context). A red-tone warning for the accent is **not built** (the rule stays a manual check, `docs/DESIGN.md` 3.2). No instance-specific favicon and no contact details beyond the website (new product functionality: owner questions of the designer, still open).
* Verified (this unit): the tests named in `docs/SECURITY.md`, section 6, and `docs/ACCEPTANCE.md`; real PNG files written by Pillow (RGB, RGBA, grey, grey and alpha, palette, one bit, 16 bit; Pillow 12.1.1 from the system Python, a one-off check outside the repository) are accepted by the validator; Adam7 interlacing is tested only with files that the test generator writes by the pass table, not with a file of a real encoder. The security reviewer has not reviewed the change.
* Open: **(1)** a browser check that an SVG with style attributes renders in `<img>` under `Content-Security-Policy: sandbox; default-src 'none'` (if not, the spec allows `style-src 'unsafe-inline'` for this one response only; not done); **(2)** a browser check that `style.setProperty` is allowed under `style-src 'self'` (the preview of the colours) and the look of the page at 375 px and 200 % zoom (designer follow-up); **(3)** the formula of the script against Python on real values (no Node.js on the development machine); **(4)** real email rendering with the new name and website; **(5)** nginx with the new location was not run (the document says so); **(6)** the security review and the decision of the project owner on the human review (D-019); **(7)** the size of the logo rows against `max_allowed_packet` and the behaviour with a MariaDB that has a smaller value; **(8)** the owner questions of the designer (favicon, contact details); **(9)** a real PNG with Adam7 interlacing and real editor SVG files (Illustrator, Inkscape "plain SVG") against the validator.
* Risks (accepted, to be reviewed): the right to change the portal settings lets a holder put attacker-chosen text and links into every page and every email (the name, the website, the privacy and legal addresses, the logo); the same trust level as the email texts (D-039): not part of any default role, one security event per change with old and new values, and the history for system administrators; a stored XSS by a file is addressed by the allow-list, `<img>` only, `nosniff`, the sandbox policy and the content type, and has a review corpus in the tests; the public logo route reads the database for every uncached request (an ETag and a day of caching limit it); a very large PNG that inflates to 128 MB (4000 by 4000 pixels, 16 bit, four channels) takes some time to check (the size of the stream is bounded by the header, not by the file).
* Sources: the owner request of 2026-10-16; `instance-settings-spec.md` of the designer (2026-10-16); `AGENTS.md`, sections 3, 6, 7 and 9; D-012, D-013, D-014, D-015, D-028, D-039, D-051, D-054, D-056, D-060, D-061; Django 6.1.2 source (`ModelAdmin`, `AdminSite`, `django.middleware.csp`, `django.utils.cache.get_conditional_response`, `Model.save` with deferred fields, `BinaryField`); Django documentation of `default_permissions` and `has_*_permission` (https://docs.djangoproject.com/en/6.1/ref/contrib/admin/); Python documentation "XML security" (https://docs.python.org/3/library/xml.html) and `zlib.decompressobj` (https://docs.python.org/3/library/zlib.html); the PNG specification (https://www.w3.org/TR/png/; chunk layout, CRC-32, Adam7 passes: known from it, **not re-read in this unit**); W3C SVG Integration (https://www.w3.org/TR/svg-integration/, as cited in `docs/DESIGN.md`); WCAG 2.2 success criteria 1.4.3, 1.4.11 and 2.5.8 (https://www.w3.org/TR/WCAG22/); PyPI page of defusedxml (https://pypi.org/pypi/defusedxml/json, checked 2026-10-16).

### D-073, Addendum 1: Corrections After the Security Review (2026-10-16)

The security reviewer read the change (no Critical or High finding; one Medium and six Low findings; nothing was run by the reviewer). This addendum **supersedes** the statements of D-073 that it names; the rest of D-073 stands. The team lead decided on each finding; the developer verified each claim in the code first (all claims were correct).

* **M-1 (Medium), fixed: the body limit was an exact path.** `BODY_LIMITS` is now a tuple of `(prefix, limit)` pairs and `middleware.body_limit(path)` takes the longest matching prefix. The prefix `/admin/accounts/portalsettings` (no trailing slash) covers every route of the model admin: the admin routes the object id as `<path:object_id>`, so `01/change/`, `+1/change/`, ` 1/change/` (`%201`), `_31/change/` (the admin's own `_XX` escape), `add/`, `history/`, `delete/` and `preview.css` reach the admin and are all limited to 3 MiB before the session and CSRF middleware read the body. `/addresses/lookup/` (4 KiB) is a prefix entry as well and behaves as before. Tests: oversized `Content-Length` for 14 spellings through the middleware alone and through the full stack with a `csrftoken` cookie and `enforce_csrf_checks` (all 413), the longest-prefix rule, the premise that the admin resolves those spellings (`resolve`), and every reversed route of the model. The wording in `docs/SECURITY.md` (section 6) and `docs/DEPLOYMENT.md` (7.3, V35) now says "every request below the prefix", not "the page". **Replaces** the "Body limit" bullet above (the key compared with the reversed URL). The `3m` nginx location (unverified, V35) stays the first bound.
* **L-1, fixed: no read and no hash for a 304 or a wrong `?v=`.** The view first reads only the type and the digest (`_logo_row` no longer selects the file) and answers a wrong or short `?v=` (404) and a matching `If-None-Match` (304) from them. Only when a body is needed it reads the file (`_logo_data`, a second query), checks the SHA-256 against the stored digest (a mismatch or an empty file is 404) and serves it; the integrity check on serve is kept. Tests: the file reader and `hashlib.sha256` are not called for the 404 and 304 cases, and no query of those requests names the data column. A conditional request whose ETag matches gets 304 from the digest alone even if the stored file were damaged; the browser already holds a file with that digest, so nothing wrong is served.
* **L-2, fixed: no `public`, no cookie, no `Vary: Cookie`.** `Cache-Control` is `max-age=86400` with `?v=` (the address changes with the file) and `no-cache` without it; `public` is gone. The view is exempt from the session: `second_factor.SESSION_FREE_VIEWS = {"instance_logo"}` and `is_session_free(request)`; `SecondFactorMiddleware.process_view` returns before it reads `request.user`, and `SessionTimeoutMiddleware.__call__` skips its check (it runs before the URL is resolved, so the path is resolved with `django.urls.resolve`; an unknown path is not free). Chain checked by reading: `SessionMiddleware` adds `Vary: Cookie` and the cookie only if the session was accessed or changed; `AuthenticationMiddleware` and `OTPMiddleware` are lazy; the timeout middleware rewrote the last activity once a minute and the second-factor middleware read `request.user`. Consequences of the exemption (intended, tested): a request for the logo neither extends nor ends a session (an expired session is not logged out by an image request; the next page does it), and an account with an incomplete second factor still gets the logo (it did before through the allow-lists). `instance_stylesheet` is **not** changed (no `public`, `no-cache`; it still reads the session; not part of the finding). Tests: no `Set-Cookie` and no `Vary: Cookie` for an anonymous, an administrator and an ordinary account whose last activity is older than the write interval, for GET and HEAD, with and without `?v=`; the premise that an ordinary page does set the cookie for the same session; the activity is not changed and the session is not ended. Not verified: a real shared cache; the documented nginx has none.
* **L-3, fixed: PNG ancillary chunks are an allow-list.** Accepted: `tRNS`, `gAMA`, `cHRM`, `sRGB`, `pHYs`, `bKGD`, each once, before the data, with the length that the specification gives (4, 32, 1, 9; `bKGD` 1, 2 or 6 by colour type; `tRNS` 2 or 6 for grey and true colour, 1 to the palette size after the palette, never for images with alpha). Everything else is rejected with the reason `png: chunk not allowed` and a new message (de-CH: export again without metadata): `tEXt`, `zTXt`, `iTXt`, `eXIf`, `iCCP`, `tIME`, `sBIT`, unknown private chunks, before or after the data. A file from an editor that writes such data has to be exported again without metadata (a usability cost that the team lead accepted). The filter byte at the start of every row (0 to 4) is checked during the inflate with the row offsets of the header (also for Adam7); the cost is a few thousand comparisons. The test that documented the acceptance of a `tEXt` chunk with markup now asserts the rejection. Verified against real files: PNGs written by Pillow 12.1.1 (system Python, a one-off check outside the repository: RGB, RGBA, grey, grey and alpha, palette with 4 bits, one bit, with `pHYs` from `dpi`, with `tRNS`, noisy images so that the encoder chooses filters, `optimize`) are accepted; a file with `tEXt` and one with `iCCP` from Pillow are rejected. The lengths and the places of the chunks follow the PNG specification (https://www.w3.org/TR/png/) **as known, not re-read in this unit**; the Pillow files confirm only the chunks that Pillow writes (`pHYs`, `tRNS`, `PLTE`), not `gAMA`, `cHRM`, `sRGB`, `bKGD`.
* **L-4, fixed and measured: pixel cap, bounded inflate.** At most **4,000,000 pixels** in total (the side limits 64 to 4000 stay; 4000 by 1000 and 2000 by 2000 pass, 4000 by 1001 does not; reason `png: too many pixels`, new message). Reason for the value: a logo of a portal is shown at a few hundred pixels; the cap makes the worst inflated size 32 MB (4 million pixels, 16 bit, four channels) instead of 128 MB. The inflate runs in steps of at most 1 MiB (was 64 KiB) and asks for at most `expected - produced + 1` bytes, so no more than one byte over the header's size is ever produced and `unconsumed_tail` is copied at most about 32 times instead of about 2000 times (the quadratic copying of the review). **Measured** (a one-off run on the development machine, five runs each, all-zero data, which inflates at the highest ratio): 4000 by 1000 RGBA 16 bit (31 kB file, 32 MB inflated) median 0.011 s, maximum 0.022 s; the same with Adam7 0.011 s; 2000 by 2000 0.011 s. The state before the change (up to 128 MB in 64 KiB steps) was **not measured**; only the bound is known. The form post of the worst file was not timed separately (the validator is the part that grows with the file; the write of a 2 MB row is not). SVG: the validator checks attribute values only by character set and, for the root, the `viewBox`; it does **not** look at `width`, `height`, `stroke-dasharray` or `stroke-dashoffset` as numbers, so no absurd-value check was added. **Accepted browser-side risk, recorded:** an SVG of at most 200 KB and 2000 elements can still cost a browser time through large dash arrays or sizes in `<img>`; it was not reviewed against a browser (the same open point (1) as above). Only a person with the right to change the portal settings can upload it, and a browser that is slow on the logo is slow on one site only.
* **L-5, fixed in the smallest consistent form: a recent login to save.** Saving the portal settings needs `second_factor.recently_authenticated(request)` (D-055: the server-side time of the login, five minutes; `RECENT_AUTHENTICATION_WINDOW`). Viewing does not. The check is the first thing in `PortalSettingsForm.clean` (the admin sets `request` in `get_form`); a session that is not recent gets a form error with the reason `recent_login`, **nothing else is validated or read, no upload is read or logged, nothing is saved**; a missing request (the form outside the admin) fails closed. On a GET of the page by someone who may change, a warning says that saving needs a new login. There is **no** re-authentication flow in the administration to reuse (the proof field with the code or the password exists only in the profile, `ReauthenticationForm`); building it into the admin form would duplicate the counters and the session end of D-062 for a page that is rarely used, so the person logs out and in again (the login is the proof). The cost: the five minutes start at the login, so a person who logged in earlier must log in again and then save within five minutes; this is stated in the message. Open for the owner: a proof field in the form instead (the `OneProofMixin` could be reused; about a day of work with tests), or a longer window for this page only. **Notification of the system administrators on a change of an address: not built; no mechanism exists** (`notify_password_changed` and the mail kinds notify the affected account only; there is no list of administrator recipients); open. The security event `PORTAL_SETTINGS_CHANGED` (long retention, old and new values) already exists. Tests: a save after the window, a missing, boolean, string or future time of the login, a staff user with only the change right: all refused with nothing saved and no event; a save at 240 s works; an old session does not reach `validate_logo` and logs no rejected upload; the warning on the page; no warning for a recent login or a view-only account.
* **L-6, accepted and recorded.** Every rejected upload writes one `portal_upload_rejected` event, so a holder of the right can fill the log; the right is already a high trust level, the event is not long-kept (the usual retention removes it), and the proxy rate limit (`limit_req`, `docs/DEPLOYMENT.md`) bounds the rate. No throttle was added. With L-5 an old session cannot write these events at all.
* **L-7, checked and recorded; no cleanup.** `default_permissions = ("view", "change")` (migration 0025) stops new databases from getting `add_portalsettings` and `delete_portalsettings`; the rows of a database that was migrated before D-073 stay (Django does not remove them; no migration of the project does). They cannot be assigned on the pages of rights: the group page offers `assignable_permissions_filter()` (only `view` and `change` of the three models) plus rights that the group already holds, and no code of the project assigns them. On the page of an account a system administrator sees the default list of all rights (`user_permissions`, system administrators only) and could assign them; it has **no effect**, because `PortalSettingsAdmin.has_add_permission` and `has_delete_permission` return `False` for everyone including system administrators (test with both rows created and assigned: 403 for add and delete). No data migration was written and none is wanted (`AGENTS.md`, section 9; a removal of the two rows is a data change on every instance and is not a convention of Django for a changed `default_permissions`: not checked in the Django documentation in this unit, so not claimed). The rows are harmless; an operator may delete them in the administration of the database if he wants a tidy list.
* **Files changed:** `leg_platform/middleware.py`, `leg_platform/presentation.py`, `leg_platform/accounts/second_factor.py`, `leg_platform/accounts/session_timeout.py`, `leg_platform/accounts/logo_images.py`, `leg_platform/accounts/portal_settings_form.py`, `leg_platform/accounts/admin.py`, `locale/de_CH/LC_MESSAGES/django.po` and `.mo` (new texts: pixel cap, metadata, recent login on save, the warning), the tests `leg_platform/tests/test_request_body_limit.py`, `leg_platform/accounts/tests/test_logo_images.py`, `leg_platform/accounts/tests/test_portal_branding.py`, and the wording in `docs/SECURITY.md` (section 6), `docs/DEPLOYMENT.md` (7.3, V35) and this entry. `leg_platform/middleware.py` is a core file, so the runner selects the full suite.
* Sources: the review text of the security reviewer (2026-10-16); Django 6.1.2 documentation and source as read for D-073 (`SessionMiddleware.process_response`: the cookie and `Vary: Cookie` only if the session was accessed or modified, re-read for this addendum; `AdminSite`/`ModelAdmin.get_urls`: `<path:object_id>/change/`; `django.urls.resolve`); Python documentation `zlib.decompressobj` and `Decompress.decompress(data, max_length)`, `unconsumed_tail` (https://docs.python.org/3/library/zlib.html); the PNG specification (https://www.w3.org/TR/png/; as known, not re-read); RFC 9111 (caching of responses with `Set-Cookie`; named by the reviewer, not re-read); D-052, D-055, D-062, D-071, D-072; `AGENTS.md`, sections 3, 7 and 9. The statement about `SessionMiddleware` was read in the installed Django source (`django/contrib/sessions/middleware.py`, `process_response`: `accessed`, `modified`, `empty`) and is shown by the tests (no cookie, no `Vary`).

### D-073, Addendum 2: Two Findings of the Browser Review (2026-10-16)

* **Preview logo (defect, seen in a real browser).** In the light panel an SVG that has only a `viewBox` (no `width`/`height`) showed nothing: the box was `inline-block` with only `max-width`/`max-height`, and such an image has no intrinsic size, so it became 0 x 0 (the PNG of the dark panel has one). `.portal-preview-logo img` in `static/css/admin-portal.css` now has an explicit box (192 x 48 px, `max-width: 100%`, `object-fit: contain`); the dark plate keeps its background. Checked that no other place needs a change: the header and the menu set `height` (48 and 32 px), the login pages set `width: 360px` or `height: 40px`, the error pages set `height: 40px`; each of these gives a viewBox-only SVG its size from the aspect ratio. Emails carry no logo. A static test pins the rule (`PreviewLogoBoxTests`). **Not run in a browser by the developer** (see the report; the check is open).
* **Saving needs a login not older than five minutes (designer finding UX-A and UX-B, approved by the team lead).** When the form is opened and the login is older than the window of D-055, a warning is shown at the top of the page before anything is typed ("To save changes you must have logged in within the last 5 minutes. Please log out and log in again first."), with a "Log out" button next to it; the same button sits below the error message after a refused save. Both buttons submit one small POST form with the CSRF token to `admin:logout` (the logout of the administration; no GET logout, which answers 405). The warning is part of `change_form.html` (it replaces the message of the messages framework of the first version), the flag comes from `PortalSettingsAdmin.change_view`, and the form sets `recent_login_refused`. The check at the save (`PortalSettingsForm.clean`) is **unchanged and authoritative**; nothing is saved and no file is read with an old login; typed values stay in the form as Django keeps them after a failed save (the chosen files cannot be kept by a browser; the warning is there so the person does not lose them).
* **Files changed:** `leg_platform/static/css/admin-portal.css`, `leg_platform/accounts/templates/admin/accounts/portalsettings/change_form.html`, `leg_platform/accounts/admin.py`, `leg_platform/accounts/portal_settings_form.py`, `locale/de_CH/LC_MESSAGES/django.po` and `.mo` (one new text, the old text of the message removed), `leg_platform/accounts/tests/test_portal_branding.py`, this entry. Tests: `scripts/test.ps1 -Area branding,admin,translations`.
* Sources: Django 6.1.2 `admin/base.html` and `admin/change_form.html` (blocks `messages`, `field_sets`; the logout form with the CSRF token and `admin:logout`, read in the installed source); HTML: the `form` attribute of `button` (a button outside its form). The browser review of the team lead (2026-10-16).

## D-074: Owner Answers of 2026-10-16: Source Reference, Imprint and Privacy Notice, AGOV, Operator, Human Review, No Review of the Translations, Structure of the Open Work

* Date: 2026-10-16
* Status: Decided (statements of the project owner). Documentation only: no code or behaviour changes. The only file outside the documents is a comment in `leg_platform/static/js/login-texts.js` (the sentence about the review of the translations is removed).
* Owner answers (2026-10-16, translated into English; the owner's German wording is not copied):
  1. **swisstopo source reference.** It goes into the privacy notice and the imprint. This is settled; the open owner question of D-066 is closed. The **content** of the imprint and of the privacy notice is **not part of the portal**: the portal only links the two addresses (portal settings, D-073). The texts are written and maintained by the user (operator, the community) elsewhere. D-022 is amended accordingly.
  2. **AGOV.** The answer is still pending; the project owner waits for the external answer (D-004). It stays "waiting", not a task of the project.
  3. **Operator.** The operator of stage and production is **Infomaniak** (owner statement; recorded as stated). Not assumed and not recorded: the product type, any technical detail, which person at Infomaniak acts, how access is arranged. The open initial-setup items O-1 to O-23 of `docs/DEPLOYMENT.md`, D-053 and D-064 stay as they are; only the question "who operates stage and production" (`docs/OPERATIONS.md`, section 6, item 19) is answered by this statement alone. **"Import account credentials" (D-049, decision 5)** were not understood by the owner. Explanation (now in `docs/OPERATIONS.md`, section 5): it is the separate database account with write rights that only the address-import worker uses to load the swisstopo address directory into the address database, separate from the application's SELECT-only account. The operator creates it on the server during the initial setup (`docs/DEPLOYMENT.md`, section 3.5); the credentials live only in the environment file of the import worker. It is therefore no longer an owner item; it stays an operator step in `docs/DEPLOYMENT.md`. *(Superseded the same day by D-075: the import account does not exist any more.)*
  4. **Human review (D-019).** The project owner does the human review himself. It stays "planned": it is not a task of an agent.
  5. **No review of the translations.** A native-speaker review of the French, Italian and English texts of the login page is not happening and not planned. Every statement that such a review is required, and every statement that the texts are drafts that await it, was deleted (README, `docs/DESIGN.md`, `leg_platform/static/js/login-texts.js`, D-056, D-069). The French, Italian and English texts and the language menu themselves are unchanged. Only the fact that the other pages are German only is kept. History is not rewritten: D-056 and D-069 carry a short dated note.
  6. **Structure of the open work in `README.md`.** The owner reads it as a sequence: (1) open now: only the AGOV answer (waiting) and the owner's own human review of the security-critical changes (D-019); (2) then the verification on a real system, planned for when nothing is left in (1), including the push for the CI run; (3) then the later items (later phases, functions not built). "Current Status" is restructured as: a short status paragraph, "Open now" and "Planned next, in this order" (A, B). The deployment gates are kept.
  7. **Statements for the operator's privacy notice.** They leave `README.md` and are collected in `docs/DEPLOYMENT.md`, section 14 ("Notes for the operator's privacy notice and imprint"): information for the operator, not a task of the project. The texts are the operator's responsibility; the section is not legal advice.
* Amended by this entry (minimal cross references): D-004 (AGOV waiting), D-022 (content is not part of the portal; the open point is closed), D-049 (decision 5), D-053 and D-064 (the operator statement), D-056 (review requirement dropped), D-066 (owner question closed), D-069 (translations and the "not decided here" list).

## D-075: The Address Directory Is an Own SQLite File, Not Backed Up; No Import Account (Owner Decisions of 2026-10-16)

* Date: 2026-10-16
* Status: Decided (project owner, explicit; relaxation approved by the team lead as a documented deviation under `AGENTS.md`, section 4). Implemented; reviewed by nobody yet (the main session starts the security review; the human review of D-019 stays open).
* **Design change of the same day (owner):** a first version of this decision stored the directory in the main MariaDB database. The owner does not want it there, because it must not be in the backup. He chose: the directory lives in its **own SQLite file on the server**, not backed up, with no extra database account and no manual creation step. Everything below describes the final design; the statements of the first version ("in the default database", "in the database dump", "no router") are void.
* Owner decisions (2026-10-16, translated): (1) the owner does not want to have to create accounts; the address directory is not important data, and if it is wrong he can simply sync again; he asked why the web application cannot have write access. (2) The directory must not be in the main database because it must not be in the backup; own SQLite file.
* Context: D-033, D-049 and D-052 isolated the directory: a second database with a `SELECT`-only account for the web process, a separate import account, a database router, an importer-only settings module and isolated SQLite test aliases. `docs/SECURITY.md`, section 7 listed this as a separation of accounts.
* Risk assessment (team lead):
  * The directory is public data of a federal service (D-033). It has no foreign key to any profile and a profile never changes because of it (D-033, D-049: the address is stored as entered; the lookup only suggests and, in the check mode, shows the not-found notice of D-066).
  * A compromised web process already controls the application database with the application account: accounts, sessions, the profile data, the mail queue. That impact is far larger than changing address suggestions. The isolation therefore protected only the integrity of suggestions and of one notice.
  * The SQLite file is local data of the server, not a secret: public data, mode 0600 for the service user, in a folder of the service user (`StateDirectory=`, mode 0700 or as systemd creates it). It holds no personal data and no credential; a copy of it discloses nothing.
  * The downloader stays out of the web process: the admin button only writes the queue row (`ImportRequest`, in the MariaDB database); the download and the import run in the worker (`process_address_imports`, or the operator command `import_address_directory`). A test (`DirectoryFileSettingsTests`) fails if a module that a web request reaches imports the importer or `requests`, or if anything but the two management commands names `process_one`, `import_directory` or the importer module.
  * Accepted residual risk: a compromised web process runs as the same user as the worker and can therefore write the file (the read-only mode of the web connection, decision 2, only protects against a mistake in the code). Effect: wrong suggestions or a wrong not-found notice; recoverable by a new import (the owner's own statement). No effect on stored data.
  * Trade-offs: SQLite is not MariaDB; D-030 covers the application database, and this file is a derived, cache-like data set that can be rebuilt from the official source. The design assumes **one host**: the web process and the worker share the file. A deployment on several hosts needs one file (and one worker) per host or a shared path; neither is planned. A file system without working file locks (some network file systems) must not hold it.
* Decision:
  1. **Storage:** the models `Directory` and `Address` live in the SQLite file, the alias `addresses` (Django's `sqlite3` backend, through `leg_platform.addresses.sqlite`, a thin subclass that creates the file as 0600 and its folder as 0700 and reports a file that cannot be prepared as a database error). `ImportRequest` (the queue row of the admin button) stays in `default`. A minimal router (`leg_platform/addresses/router.py`) sends each model to its alias and lets `migrate` create only the directory tables in the file and everything else only in the application database. Both the web process (reads) and the worker (writes) use the same alias and settings; there is no second account and no second settings module.
  2. **Read-only web connection (decision: one alias, not two).** `leg_platform.wsgi` and `leg_platform.asgi` call `storage.mark_web_process()`; every connection to the directory that such a process opens runs `PRAGMA query_only = ON` (a `connection_created` handler). The worker, the operator commands, the seed and `migrate` do not call it and write. A separate read-only alias (`mode=ro`) was considered and rejected: the file must exist for `mode=ro`, `migrate` needs the writer alias anyway, and in the tests two connections to one file would need `TransactionTestCase` everywhere (the earlier mirror alias showed this); the gain is the same, because both processes run as the same user. Verified: a test opens a connection under the web flag and shows that insert, update and delete fail with "readonly".
  3. **File path:** the optional variable `ADDRESS_DIRECTORY_FILE` (validated in `environment.py`: absolute, not inside the repository, no NUL or other control characters, not a folder). Defaults: outside `APP_ENV=local` `/var/lib/leg-platform/address-directory.sqlite3` (the systemd units get `StateDirectory=leg-platform`, so no manual `mkdir`); with `APP_ENV=local` `~/.leg-platform-dev/address-directory.sqlite3` (`pathlib`, never inside the repository).
  4. **SQLite settings:** `journal_mode = WAL`, `synchronous = NORMAL`, `busy_timeout` 20 s (`OPTIONS["timeout"]` and `PRAGMA`), `transaction_mode = IMMEDIATE`. Checked in Django 6.1.2 (`django/db/backends/sqlite3/base.py`: `init_command`, `transaction_mode`, the connection keyword arguments, a `file:` name works because the backend always opens URIs). WAL lets a lookup read while the import writes; NORMAL cannot corrupt the file in WAL mode (a power loss can lose the last commits, which a repeatable import tolerates).
  5. **Search keys instead of `LIKE`:** SQLite compares in binary order and its `LIKE` ignores case only for ASCII. `Address` has three folded key columns (`street_key`, `house_number_key`, `locality_key`: lower case without accents, the same `fold` as the approximate matcher, `leg_platform/addresses/keys.py`), filled on save and in `bulk_create`. A prefix is a range `key >= prefix AND key < prefix + U+10FFFF`; the postal code prefix is a range too, a complete postal code an equality. The behaviour of the MariaDB collation (case and accents ignored) is kept; the answer shows the spelling of the directory, ordered by the street key. Two indexes: `addr_generation_postal_street` (generation, postal code, street key, street, house number key) for the check, the candidate streets and the addresses of one street; `addr_generation_street` (generation, street key, street, house number, postal code, town) for the suggestions. A test runs `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN` on the real statements and asserts `SEARCH ... USING INDEX` and no table scan; the candidate query has no `ORDER BY` because with it SQLite walked the whole generation (measured: 85 ms with 400,000 rows). After an import the importer runs `ANALYZE` (with `analysis_limit`) so that the planner knows the data (with the statistics it picks the postal code index for a complete postal code).
  6. **Import into SQLite:** the new generation is inserted in transactions of 20,000 rows (`importer.CHUNK_ROWS`) and stays invisible; one short final transaction switches `Directory.generation`. A failure deletes the half-loaded rows; a crash leaves them (invisible) for the next import to delete. A lease on the `Directory` row (`import_token`, `import_lease_until`, 6 hours, renewed with every chunk) keeps two importers (the worker and the operator command) from running at the same time; the queue state `RUNNING` stays as it was. After the import the log is checkpointed (`wal_checkpoint(TRUNCATE)`).
  7. **Retention:** after a successful import only the new (current) generation and the previous one are kept; every older generation is deleted, per generation in batches of 5,000 rows (`importer.PRUNE_BATCH_SIZE`, a judgement call, not measured), each batch its own short statement. A failure of the cleanup is logged and does not fail the import; the next successful import removes what is left. Nothing is deleted when the import fails, the catalogue date is older or the checksum is unchanged. The freed pages are reused by the next import; the file does not shrink, so its size stays at the peak of **three generations** during an import (about 0.9 GB per generation, see below). `VACUUM` was not chosen (it needs the free space of a whole copy).
  8. **No backup, no creation step:** the file is not part of any backup (backup = the MariaDB database and the environment file). It is created when the first process opens it (the folder by `StateDirectory=`), the tables by `migrate addresses --database=addresses` (`setup_instance` will run it), and filled by the first automatic import when the worker first starts. A missing, empty or unreadable file means "no suggestions", silently for the people who type (D-066); the operator page of the administration shows the status.
  9. **Removed (from the first version of this decision and from D-033, D-049 and D-052):** the import account and its credentials, `import.env`, `leg_platform.import_settings`, `leg_platform/address_configuration.py`, the `SELECT`-only account, the variables `ADDRESS_DB_*` and `ADDRESS_IMPORT_DB_*`, `local_directory_settings.py`, `importer.require_writer()`, `READ_ALIAS`/`WRITE_ALIAS`. The importer service uses `app.env` like the mail worker.
  10. **Migration:** `leg_platform/addresses/migrations/0001_initial.py` was edited in place (not deployed anywhere: README deployment gates, D-053) to add the key columns, the lease columns and the two indexes. No data migration.
  11. **Tests:** the address tests run against a temporary SQLite file (`leg_platform/test_settings.py`: a folder of this run in the system temporary folder, outside the repository, removed at exit; Django's test database path). The test classes of `leg_platform/accounts/tests/` that touch the directory declare `databases = {"default", "addresses"}`. New tests: aliases and router, `migrate` per alias (a child process creates only the two directory tables), the path validation (`test_environment.py`), the file mode, the pragmas, the read-only web connection, the query plans, case and accent insensitive prefix search, `LIKE` wildcards as plain characters, chunked import, crash safety, the lease, retention.
* Estimates (**unmeasured on a server**): the real directory has about 3.3 million rows (D-049). Measured on this development machine with synthetic rows: 600,000 rows imported through the real importer path in 133 s (about 4,500 rows per second including the CSV parse) and 161 MB; extrapolated: about 12 minutes of import time (more with longer real strings and a slower disk) and about 0.9 GB per generation (table plus the two indexes), so about 2 GB at rest and 3 GB at the peak of an import, plus the temporary download (about 143 MB) and the write-ahead log (empty after the import).
* Not verified: behaviour with the real directory (the query plans, the import time, the file size), on Linux (the file modes were tested on Windows: the assertions for 0600 and 0700 run only on POSIX), and with SQLite versions older than 3.50 (the installed one; Django 6.1 requires at least 3.31; `analysis_limit` needs 3.32 and is ignored before); the minimum CI job (MariaDB 10.11, Python 3.12) has not run for this change.
* Open points (reported to the team lead): (a) the forced re-import of the manual request (D-076) exists, so "sync again" repairs a damaged directory; (b) the checksum shortcut of the automatic attempt is unchanged; (c) `setup_instance` runs `migrate addresses --database=addresses` since D-076; (d) `manage.py check` with the default settings opens the directory file (creating an empty one) because Django 6.1 checks every database; this is harmless.
* **Superseded text of the first version of this entry** (kept short for the history): the directory in the default database, no router, the directory in the database dump, the lookup through the default database, the retention through `DELETE` batches on MariaDB, and the tests on the MariaDB test database.
